Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

1. 样本数据6,8,4,5,12的中位数为（ ）
A. 5
B. 6
C. 8
D. 9

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要找出给定样本数据的中位数，需按照以下步骤操作：

1. **将数据排序**：中位数的定义是基于有序序列的中间值。将给定的数据 `6, 8, 4, 5, 12` 按从小到大顺序排列：
   `4, 5, 6, 8, 12`

2. **确定数据个数**：该样本共包含 $n=5$ 个数据。

3. **寻找中间位置**：由于数据个数为奇数，中位数位于第 $\frac{n+1}{2}$ 个位置。
   计算得：$\frac{5+1}{2} = 3$，即第3个数据点即为中位数。

4. **取值**：在排序后的序列 `4, 5, 6, 8, 12` 中，第3个数为 `6`。

因此，该样本数据的中位数为 $6$，对应选项 **B**。

\boxed{B}

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

2. 已知平面向量\vec{a}，\vec{b}不共线，且2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}，则（ ）
A. x=2, y=-3
B. x=-2, y=3
C. x=2, y=3
D. x=-2, y=-3

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
这道题考查的是平面向量（线性无关向量）的性质。

**1. 分析题目条件**
题目给出的方程为：
$$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$$
题目明确指出平面向量 $\vec{a}$ 和 $\vec{b}$ **不共线**。

**2. 利用向量不共线的性质**
在平面几何中，如果两个向量 $\vec{a}$ 和 $\vec{b}$ 不共线，那么它们构成一个基底（基）。根据线性组合的唯一性定理，如果两个向量不共线，那么它们的线性组合表示的向量相等，当且仅当对应向量的系数相等。
或者，我们可以将方程整理为：
$$(2 - x)\vec{a} + (y + 3)\vec{b} = \vec{0}$$
由于 $\vec{a}$ 和 $\vec{b}$ 不共线（线性无关），要使它们的线性组合等于零向量，必须使得每一个系数都为 0。

**3. 建立方程组并求解**
根据上述性质，我们可以对应系数相等：
*   $\vec{a}$ 的系数：$2 = x$
*   $\vec{b}$ 的系数：$y = -3$

解得：
$$x = 2, \quad y = -3$$

**4. 对照选项**
我们得到的解是 $x=2, y=-3$。
观察选项：
A. $x=2, y=-3$
B. $x=-2, y=3$
C. $x=2, y=3$
D. $x=-2, y=-3$

结果与选项 A 完全一致。

**结论**
因此，正确答案是 A。

\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's my thought process for solving this vector problem:

1.  **Analyze the Request:** The user wants me to solve a specific math problem involving vectors. The problem asks to find the values of scalars $x$ and $y$ given a vector equation, assuming vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear (not parallel). The final answer needs to be boxed.

2.  **Examine the Given Information:**
    *   $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are planar vectors (in a 2D space).
    *   $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are *not collinear* (linearly independent). This is a crucial piece of information. It means neither vector is a scalar multiple of the other.
    *   The equation is: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.

3.  **Analyze the Equation:**
    *   LHS (Left Hand Side): $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b}$
    *   RHS (Right Hand Side): $x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$
    *   This equation equates two linear combinations of the same two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$.

4.  **Recall the Linear Independence Property of Vectors:**
    *   If two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear (linearly independent), then the expression $k_1\vec{a} + k_2\vec{b} = \vec{0}$ holds if and only if $k_1 = 0$ and $k_2 = 0$.
    *   More generally, if $k_1\vec{a} + k_2\vec{b} = m_1\vec{a} + m_2\vec{b}$, then the coefficients of the same basis vectors must be equal. That is, $k_1 = m_1$ and $k_2 = m_2$.
    *   Why? Because we can rearrange the equation to $(k_1 - m_1)\vec{a} + (k_2 - m_2)\vec{b} = \vec{0}$. Since $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are linearly independent, the coefficients must vanish.

5.  **Apply the Property to the Specific Problem:**
    *   Rearrange the given equation to group $\vec{a}$ terms on one side and $\vec{b}$ terms on the other, or just compare coefficients directly.
    *   Method 1: Rearrangement
        $$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$$
        Subtract $x\vec{a}$ from both sides:
        $$(2 - x)\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = -3\vec{b}$$
        Add $3\vec{b}$ to both sides:
        $$(2 - x)\vec{a} + (y + 3)\vec{b} = \vec{0}$$
    *   Since $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are not collinear, they are linearly independent. Therefore, for the linear combination to equal the zero vector, the coefficients must be zero.
        $$2 - x = 0$$
        $$y + 3 = 0$$

    *   Method 2: Direct Coefficient Comparison
        Since $\{\vec{a}, \vec{b}\}$ forms a basis for the plane (because they are non-collinear), the representation of any vector in this basis is unique.
        Comparing the coefficient of $\vec{a}$: $2 = x$.
        Comparing the coefficient of $\vec{b}$: $y = -3$.

6.  **Solve for $x$ and $y$:**
    *   From $2 - x = 0 \implies x = 2$.
    *   From $y + 3 = 0 \implies y = -3$.

7.  **Check the Options:**
    *   A. $x=2, y=-3$ (Matches)
    *   B. $x=-2, y=3$
    *   C. $x=2, y=3$
    *   D. $x=-2, y=-3$

8.  **Construct the Final Output:**
    *   State the condition: Vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear.
    *   State the property: Coefficients of linearly independent vectors must be equal on both sides of the equation.
    *   Set up the equations:
        *   Coefficient of $\vec{a}$: $2 = x$
        *   Coefficient of $\vec{b}$: $y = -3$
    *   Conclude the values: $x=2, y=-3$.
    *   Select the corresponding option: A.
    *   Format the answer as requested.

9.  **Refining the Explanation:**
    *   Start by writing down the equation.
    *   Mention the definition of non-collinear vectors (linearly independent).
    *   Explain that if $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are not collinear, then $m\vec{a} + n\vec{b} = p\vec{a} + q\vec{b}$ implies $m=p$ and $n=q$.
    *   Apply this to the given equation $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.
    *   Compare coefficients for $\vec{a}$: $2 = x$.
    *   Compare coefficients for $\vec{b}$: $y = -3$.
    *   State the result clearly.

    1.  **Analyze the Vector Equation**: The given equation is $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$. This equation involves two vectors, $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$, which are described as "planar vectors" (平面向量) and "not collinear" (不共线).

    2.  **Understand "Not Collinear"**: In vector algebra, two vectors are not collinear if and only if they are linearly independent. This means neither vector can be expressed as a scalar multiple of the other. A crucial property of linearly independent vectors is that any vector can be uniquely represented as a linear combination of them (if they form a basis). Specifically, if $k_1\vec{a} + k_2\vec{b} = m_1\vec{a} + m_2\vec{b}$, then the coefficients must satisfy $k_1 = m_1$ and $k_2 = m_2$.

    3.  **Rearrange the Equation**: To apply this property, we can rearrange the terms in the given equation to group the vectors on one side or simply compare the coefficients on both sides.
        Let's move all terms to the left side:
        $(2 - x)\vec{a} + (y + 3)\vec{b} = \vec{0}$.

    4.  **Apply Linear Independence Property**: Since $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are not collinear, the only way for their linear combination to equal the zero vector ($\vec{0}$) is if both of their coefficients are equal to zero.
        Therefore, we have the system of equations:
        $2 - x = 0$
        $y + 3 = 0$

    5.  **Solve for $x$ and $y$**:
        From $2 - x = 0$, we get $x = 2$.
        From $y + 3 = 0$, we get $y = -3$.

    6.  **Compare with Options**:
        We found $x = 2$ and $y = -3$.
        Looking at the given options:
        A. $x=2, y=-3$
        B. $x=-2, y=3$
        C. $x=2, y=3$
        D. $x=-2, y=-3$
        Our result matches Option A.

    7.  **Conclusion**: The correct choice is A.

Prompt

3. 已知集合A={sin\frac{7\pi}{6}, cos\frac{5\pi}{3}, tan\frac{5\pi}{4}}, B={-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}，则A \cap B = （ ）
A. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}}
B. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1}
C. {-\frac{1}{2}, 1}
D. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
逐步推理如下：

1. **计算集合 $A$ 中各元素的具体值**：
   - $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6}$：$\frac{7\pi}{6}$ 位于第三象限，正弦值为负，参考角为 $\frac{\pi}{6}$，故 $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = -\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = -\frac{1}{2}$。
   - $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3}$：$\frac{5\pi}{3}$ 位于第四象限，余弦值为正，参考角为 $\frac{\pi}{3}$，故 $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3} = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}$。
   - $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4}$：$\frac{5\pi}{4}$ 位于第三象限，正切值为正，参考角为 $\frac{\pi}{4}$，故 $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$。
   
   因此，集合 $A = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

2. **求交集 $A \cap B$**：
   已知集合 $B = \left\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。
   交集是指两个集合中相同的元素。对比 $A$ 和 $B$：
   - $-\frac{1}{2}$ 同时属于 $A$ 和 $B$；
   - $1$ 同时属于 $A$ 和 $B$；
   - $\frac{1}{2}$ 仅在 $A$ 中，$-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ 仅在 $B$ 中。
   
   故 $A \cap B = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

3. **对照选项**：
   计算结果对应选项 C。

\boxed{C}

gaokao_000_003 ✓ D D Y Y 2290 84.5 27.1 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

4. 曲线y=5x+8lnx在点(1,5)处的切线方程为（ ）
A. y=3x+2
B. y=5x
C. y=8x-3
D. y=13x-8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **求函数的导数**：  
   曲线在某点处的切线斜率等于函数在该点的导数值。  
   已知函数 $y = 5x + 8\ln x$，求导得：  
   $$y' = \frac{d}{dx}(5x) + \frac{d}{dx}(8\ln x) = 5 + \frac{8}{x}$$

2. **计算切点处的斜率**：  
   将切点的横坐标 $x = 1$ 代入导数中：  
   $$y'(1) = 5 + \frac{8}{1} = 13$$  
   故切线的斜率 $k = 13$。

3. **写出切线方程**：  
   利用点斜式方程 $y - y_0 = k(x - x_0)$，代入已知点 $(x_0, y_0) = (1, 5)$ 和斜率 $k = 13$：  
   $$y - 5 = 13(x - 1)$$  
   化简得：  
   $$y - 5 = 13x - 13 \quad \Rightarrow \quad y = 13x - 8$$

4. **对照选项**：  
   所得方程 $y = 13x - 8$ 与选项 D 完全一致。

最终答案：\boxed{D}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question:** 曲线 $y=5x+8\ln x$ 在点 $(1,5)$ 处的切线方程为（ ）

   - Function: $y = f(x) = 5x + 8\ln x$
   - Point: $(x_0, y_0) = (1, 5)$ (Check if it's on the curve: $f(1) = 5(1) + 8\ln 1 = 5 + 0 = 5$. Yes.)

3.  **Compute the Derivative:**
   - $f(x) = 5x + 8\ln x$
   - $f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(5x) + \frac{d}{dx}(8\ln x) = 5 + 8 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 5 + \frac{8}{x}$

4.  **Evaluate the Derivative at the Given Point:**
   - $x_0 = 1$

   - So, the slope of the tangent line at $(1, 5)$ is $m = 13$.

5.  **Formulate the Tangent Line Equation:**
   - Point-slope form: $y - y_0 = m(x - x_0)$
   - $y - 5 = 13(x - 1)$
   - $y - 5 = 13x - 13$
   - $y = 13x - 13 + 5$
   - $y = 13x - 8$

gaokao_000_004 ✓ D D Y Y 4801 83.7 57.4 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

5. 已知抛物线C_1:y^2=2p_1x (p_1>0)和C_2:x^2=2p_2y (p_2>0)均经过点(4,8)，则C_1的焦点与C_2的焦点之间的距离为（ ）
A. 12
B. 4\sqrt{5}
C. 6
D. \frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要找到两个抛物线 $C_1$ 和 $C_2$ 的焦点之间的距离，我们需要先根据题目给出的条件求出两个抛物线的方程参数，从而确定各自的焦点坐标，最后利用距离公式计算结果。

**第一步：求抛物线 $C_1$ 的焦点**

1.  **分析方程**：抛物线 $C_1$ 的方程为 $y^2 = 2p_1x$ ($p_1 > 0)$。这是开口向右的标准抛物线方程。
    *   对于形式 $y^2 = 2px$ 的抛物线，其焦点坐标为 $(\frac{p}{2}, 0)$。
    *   因此，$C_1$ 的焦点 $F_1$ 的坐标为 $(\frac{p_1}{2}, 0)$。

2.  **求参数 $p_1$**：已知抛物线 $C_1$ 经过点 $(4, 8)$。将点 $(4, 8)$ 代入方程：
    $$8^2 = 2p_1 \cdot 4$$
    $$64 = 8p_1$$
    $$p_1 = 8$$

3.  **确定焦点 $F_1$**：
    $$F_1 = (\frac{8}{2}, 0) = (4, 0)$$

**第二步：求抛物线 $C_2$ 的焦点**

1.  **分析方程**：抛物线 $C_2$ 的方程为 $x^2 = 2p_2y$ ($p_2 > 0$)。这是开口向上的标准抛物线方程。
    *   对于形式 $x^2 = 2py$ 的抛物线，其焦点坐标为 $(0, \frac{p}{2})$。
    *   因此，$C_2$ 的焦点 $F_2$ 的坐标为 $(0, \frac{p_2}{2})$。

2.  **求参数 $p_2$**：已知抛物线 $C_2$ 经过点 $(4, 8)$。将点 $(4, 8)$ 代入方程：
    $$4^2 = 2p_2 \cdot 8$$
    $$16 = 16p_2$$
    $$p_2 = 1$$

3.  **确定焦点 $F_2$**：
    $$F_2 = (0, \frac{1}{2}) = (0, 0.5)$$

**第三步：计算两焦点之间的距离**

1.  两焦点的坐标分别为 $F_1(4, 0)$ 和 $F_2(0, \frac{1}{2})$。
2.  利用两点距离公式 $d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}$ 计算距离：
    $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{(0 - 4)^2 + (\frac{1}{2} - 0)^2}$$
    $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{(-4)^2 + (\frac{1}{2})^2}$$
    $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{16 + \frac{1}{4}}$$
    $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{\frac{64}{4} + \frac{1}{4}}$$
    $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{\frac{65}{4}}$$
    $$|F_1F_2| = \frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}$$

**结论**

C_1的焦点与C_2的焦点之间的距离为 $\frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}$。
这与选项 D 相符。

\boxed{D}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to find the distance between the foci of two parabolas, $C_1$ and $C_2$, given that both pass through the point $(4, 8)$ and their equations are provided in standard forms.

2.  **Analyze Parabola $C_1$:**
    *   Equation: $y^2 = 2p_1 x$ with $p_1 > 0$.
    *   This is a standard parabola opening to the right.
    *   Standard form: $y^2 = 4ax$ or $y^2 = 2px$. Here $2p_1 = 4a$, so $a = p_1/2$.
    *   The focus of a parabola $y^2 = 2px$ is at $(p/2, 0)$. Let's verify this.
        *   Vertex at $(0,0)$.
        *   Directrix $x = -p/2$.
        *   Focus is at $(p/2, 0)$.
    *   Given condition: $C_1$ passes through $(4, 8)$.
    *   Substitute the point into the equation to find $p_1$:
        $$8^2 = 2p_1 (4)$$
        $$64 = 8p_1$$
        $$p_1 = 8$$
    *   Now, determine the coordinates of the focus of $C_1$ (let's call it $F_1$).
        *   Focus $F_1 = (p_1/2, 0) = (8/2, 0) = (4, 0)$.

3.  **Analyze Parabola $C_2$:**
    *   Equation: $x^2 = 2p_2 y$ with $p_2 > 0$.
    *   This is a standard parabola opening upwards.
    *   Standard form: $x^2 = 4by$. Here $2p_2 = 4b$, so $b = p_2/2$.
    *   The focus of a parabola $x^2 = 2py$ is at $(0, p/2)$.
    *   Given condition: $C_2$ passes through $(4, 8)$.
    *   Substitute the point into the equation to find $p_2$:
        $$4^2 = 2p_2 (8)$$
        $$16 = 16p_2$$
        $$p_2 = 1$$
    *   Now, determine the coordinates of the focus of $C_2$ (let's call it $F_2$).
        *   Focus $F_2 = (0, p_2/2) = (0, 1/2) = (0, 0.5)$.

4.  **Calculate the Distance between Foci:**
    *   Coordinates of $F_1$: $(4, 0)$.
    *   Coordinates of $F_2$: $(0, \frac{1}{2})$.
    *   Use the distance formula $d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}$.
    *   $d = \sqrt{(0 - 4)^2 + (\frac{1}{2} - 0)^2}$
    *   $d = \sqrt{(-4)^2 + (\frac{1}{2})^2}$
    *   $d = \sqrt{16 + \frac{1}{4}}$
    *   $d = \sqrt{\frac{64}{4} + \frac{1}{4}}$
    *   $d = \sqrt{\frac{65}{4}}$
    *   $d = \frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}$

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

6. 已知函数f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}的最大值为1，则a = （ ）
A. \frac{1}{2}
B. 1
C. \frac{3}{2}
D. 2

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
为了解决这个问题，我们需要找到使得函数 $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$ 的最大值为 1 的常数 $a$。

### 1. 求函数的导数
首先，计算 $f(x)$ 关于 $x$ 的导数 $f'(x)$。根据商法则：
$$f'(x) = \frac{(x+2)'(e^x+a) - (x+2)(e^x+a)'}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{1 \cdot (e^x+a) - (x+2)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}$$
整理分子，得到：
$$f'(x) = \frac{e^x + a - xe^x - 2e^x}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{a - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x+a)^2}$$

### 2. 建立极值方程组
函数在最大值点处满足两个条件：导数为 0，且函数值等于 1。设最大值点为 $x_0$：
1.  **导数为 0：** $a - e^{x_0}(1+x_0) = 0 \implies a = e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$
2.  **函数值为 1：** $\frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0}+a} = 1 \implies x_0+2 = e^{x_0} + a$

### 3. 求解 $a$ 的值
将方程 (1) 代入方程 (2)：
$$x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$$
$$x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0}(1 + 1 + x_0)$$
$$x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0}(x_0 + 2)$$
$$(x_0 + 2)(1 - e^{x_0}) = 0$$

此方程有两个解：
*   **情况 1：$x_0 + 2 = 0 \implies x_0 = -2$**
    代入 (1) 得 $a = e^{-2}(1-2) = -e^{-2}$。但分母 $e^x + a$ 在 $x=-2$ 处为零，函数无定义，故舍去。
*   **情况 2：$1 - e^{x_0} = 0 \implies e^{x_0} = 1 \implies x_0 = 0$**
    代入 (1) 得 $a = e^0(1+0) = 1$。

### 4. 验证最大值
当 $a=1$ 时，函数为 $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$。
*   在 $x=0$ 处，$f(0) = \frac{2}{2} = 1$。
*   分析导数 $f'(x) = \frac{1 - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x+1)^2}$：在 $x=0$ 左侧导数为正，在右侧导数为负，表明 $x=0$ 确实是全局最大值点。

因此，$a=1$，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The problem asks for the value of the constant $a$ such that the function $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$ has a maximum value of 1.

2.  **Analyze the Function:**
    *   $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$.
    *   Domain: The denominator cannot be zero. $e^x + a \neq 0$. Since $e^x > 0$, if $a \ge 0$, the denominator is always positive. If $a < 0$, we need to consider where $e^x \neq -a$. However, usually in such multiple-choice contexts involving maxima, we assume the function is defined on $\mathbb{R}$ or the relevant domain where the maximum exists. Let's check the options: $a \in \{1/2, 1, 3/2, 2\}$. All are positive. So the domain is $\mathbb{R}$.

3.  **Find the Derivative:**
    To find the maximum, I need to find the critical points by setting the derivative $f'(x)$ to zero.
    Let $u = x+2$ and $v = e^x + a$.
    Then $f(x) = \frac{u}{v}$.
    Using the quotient rule: $f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}$.
    $u' = 1$.
    $v' = e^x$.
    $f'(x) = \frac{1 \cdot (e^x + a) - (x+2) \cdot e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
    $f'(x) = \frac{e^x + a - xe^x - 2e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
    $f'(x) = \frac{a - e^x - xe^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
    $f'(x) = \frac{a - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x + a)^2}$

4.  **Set Derivative to Zero:**
    $f'(x) = 0 \implies a - e^x(1+x) = 0$
    $e^x(1+x) = a$

5.  **Analyze the Maximum Condition:**
    The problem states the maximum value is 1.
    This implies two things:
    a) There exists a critical point $x_0$ such that $f(x_0) = 1$.
    b) This critical point corresponds to a local maximum (or global maximum).

    Let's look at condition (a): $f(x_0) = 1$.
    $\frac{x_0 + 2}{e^{x_0} + a} = 1$
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + a$
    $x_0 + 2 - a = e^{x_0}$

    We also have the condition from the derivative:
    $e^{x_0}(1+x_0) = a$
    $e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0} = a$

    Now we have a system of two equations with two variables ($x_0$ and $a$):
    1) $e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2 - a$
    2) $e^{x_0}(1+x_0) = a$

    Let's substitute (1) into (2) or manipulate them.
    From (1), $a = e^{x_0} - x_0 - 2$.
    Substitute this expression for $a$ into equation (2):
    $e^{x_0}(1+x_0) = e^{x_0} - x_0 - 2$
    $e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0} = e^{x_0} - x_0 - 2$
    Subtract $e^{x_0}$ from both sides:
    $x_0 e^{x_0} = -x_0 - 2$
    $x_0 e^{x_0} + x_0 + 2 = 0$
    $x_0(e^{x_0} + 1) = -2$

    Wait, let me re-check the algebra.
    Eq 1: $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + a \implies a = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0}$
    Eq 2: $a = e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$
    Set equal:
    $x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0} = e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$
    $x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0} = e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0}$
    $x_0 + 2 = 2e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0}$
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0}(2 + x_0)$

    This looks much cleaner.
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0}(x_0 + 2)$
    $(x_0 + 2) - e^{x_0}(x_0 + 2) = 0$
    $(x_0 + 2)(1 - e^{x_0}) = 0$

    So, either $x_0 + 2 = 0$ or $1 - e^{x_0} = 0$.
    Case 1: $x_0 = -2$.
    If $x_0 = -2$, let's find $a$.
    Using Eq 1: $a = (-2) + 2 - e^{-2} = -e^{-2}$.
    But $a$ must be positive (based on options). Or even if not, let's check the derivative condition.
    $a = e^{-2}(1 + (-2)) = -e^{-2}$. Consistent.
    However, if $a = -1/e^2$, the denominator $e^x + a$ could be zero at $x = -2$? No, $e^{-2} - 1/e^2 = 0$. So $x=-2$ is a vertical asymptote. The function isn't defined there. So this case is invalid.

    Case 2: $1 - e^{x_0} = 0 \implies e^{x_0} = 1 \implies x_0 = 0$.
    If $x_0 = 0$, let's find $a$.
    Using Eq 1: $a = 0 + 2 - e^0 = 2 - 1 = 1$.
    Using Eq 2: $a = e^0(1+0) = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$.
    Both give $a = 1$.

    So, $x_0 = 0$ and $a = 1$.

6.  **Verify the Maximum:**
    We found a candidate $a=1$ where the function equals 1 at $x=0$.
    Let's check if it's actually a maximum.
    $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$.
    $f(0) = \frac{2}{1+1} = 1$.
    Check derivative sign around $x=0$.
    $f'(x) = \frac{1 - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x+1)^2}$.
    Let $g(x) = 1 - e^x(1+x)$. We need to check the sign of $g(x)$ around 0.
    $g(0) = 1 - 1(1) = 0$.
    $g'(x) = - [e^x(1+x) + e^x(1)] = -e^x(1 + x + 1) = -e^x(x+2)$.
    For $x$ near 0 (specifically $x > -2$), $g'(x) < 0$.
    So $g(x)$ is decreasing.
    If $x < 0$ (and close to 0), $g(x) > g(0) = 0$, so $f'(x) > 0$.
    If $x > 0$, $g(x) < g(0) = 0$, so $f'(x) < 0$.
    Thus, $x=0$ is a local maximum.

    Is it the global maximum?
    Check limits.
    $\lim_{x \to -\infty} f(x) = \frac{-\infty}{0+1} = -\infty$.
    $\lim_{x \to \infty} f(x) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x+2}{e^x} = 0$ (by L'Hopital or growth rates).
    Since the function goes from $-\infty$ to a peak at $x=0$ with value 1, and then approaches 0 from above (since for $x > 0$, $x+2 > 0$ and $e^x+1 > 0$), the maximum value is indeed 1.

    Wait, let's double check the sign of $f(x)$ for $x > 0$.
    $x+2 > 0$, $e^x+1 > 0$, so $f(x) > 0$.
    For $x < -2$, $x+2 < 0$, $e^x+1 > 0$, so $f(x) < 0$.
    So the function is negative for $x < -2$ and positive for $x > -2$.
    The maximum must be in the positive region.
    We found the critical point at $x=0$ gives value 1.
    Are there other critical points?
    $f'(x)=0 \iff 1 = e^x(1+x)$.
    Let $h(x) = e^x(1+x)$.
    $h'(x) = e^x(1+x) + e^x = e^x(x+2)$.
    Critical point of $h(x)$ is at $x=-2$.
    $h(-2) = e^{-2}(-1) = -1/e^2 < 0$.
    For $x > -2$, $h'(x) > 0$, so $h(x)$ is increasing.
    Range of $h(x)$ for $x \in (-2, \infty)$ is $(-1/e^2, \infty)$.
    We want to solve $h(x) = 1$.
    Since $1 > 0 > -1/e^2$, there is exactly one solution $x_0$ in $(-2, \infty)$.
    We found $x_0 = 0$ because $e^0(1+0) = 1$.
    So the critical point is unique.
    Since $h(x)$ is increasing, $f'(x)$ changes sign from positive to negative at $x_0=0$.
    So $x=0$ is the unique global maximum.
    And $f(0) = 1$.
    So $a=1$ works.

    If $f(x)=1$, $x+2 = e^x + 1.5 \implies x+0.5 = e^x$.

7.  **Rigorous Derivation Steps:**
    Let's write down the steps clearly.
    Function: $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$.
    To find max value, calculate derivative.
    $f'(x) = \frac{1(e^x+a) - (x+2)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{a - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x+a)^2}$.
    Critical points occur when $f'(x) = 0$, assuming denominator $\neq 0$.
    $a - e^x(1+x) = 0 \implies a = e^x(1+x)$.
    Let $x_0$ be the location of the maximum.
    Then $f(x_0) = 1$.
    $\frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0}+a} = 1 \implies x_0+2 = e^{x_0} + a$.
    Substitute $a = e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$ into this equation:
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + e^{x_0}(1+x_0)$
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0}$
    $x_0 + 2 = 2e^{x_0} + x_0 e^{x_0}$
    $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0}(2 + x_0)$
    $(x_0 + 2) - e^{x_0}(x_0 + 2) = 0$
    $(x_0 + 2)(1 - e^{x_0}) = 0$.

    This gives two possibilities for the location of the maximum $x_0$:
    1.  $x_0 + 2 = 0 \implies x_0 = -2$.
        If $x_0 = -2$, calculate $a$.
        $a = e^{-2}(1 + (-2)) = -e^{-2}$.
        However, if $a = -1/e^2$, the denominator $e^x + a$ is zero at $x = -2$ (since $e^{-2} - 1/e^2 = 0$).
        The function is undefined at $x=-2$.
        Also, usually "maximum value of function" implies a defined real-valued function on an interval. If the domain excludes -2, and -2 is the critical point, it's an asymptote, not a max value in the usual sense (or value would be $\infty$).
        Also, options are positive. $a$ cannot be negative.
        So $x_0 = -2$ is rejected.

    2.  $1 - e^{x_0} = 0 \implies e^{x_0} = 1 \implies x_0 = 0$.
        If $x_0 = 0$, calculate $a$.
        $a = e^0(1+0) = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$.
        Check if this is a maximum.
        $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$.
        $f'(x) = \frac{1 - e^x(1+x)}{(e^x+1)^2}$.
        Check second derivative or sign change.
        Numerator $N(x) = 1 - e^x - xe^x$.
        $N'(x) = -e^x - (e^x + xe^x) = -e^x(2+x)$.
        For $x$ near 0 (specifically $x > -2$), $N'(x) < 0$.
        $N(0) = 0$.
        So for $x < 0$ (close to 0), $N(x) > 0 \implies f'(x) > 0$.
        For $x > 0$, $N(x) < 0 \implies f'(x) < 0$.
        Function increases then decreases.
        $x=0$ is a local maximum.
        Value $f(0) = \frac{0+2}{1+1} = 1$.
        Check global behavior.
        $\lim_{x \to -\infty} f(x) = \frac{-\infty}{1} = -\infty$.
        $\lim_{x \to \infty} f(x) = 0$.
        The function is continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}$ (since $a=1>0$).
        So the local maximum at $x=0$ is the global maximum.
        Max value is 1.
        This matches the problem statement.

    So $a=1$.

    Let's double check if there's any tricky constraint or domain issue.
    $a$ is a parameter.
    If $a=1$, $e^x+1 \ge 1 > 0$. Denominator never zero.
    Function defined on $\mathbb{R}$.
    Calculations hold.

    Let's check the logic for $x_0 = -2$ again.
    If $a = -e^{-2}$, domain is $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{-2\}$.
    At $x=-2$, vertical asymptote.
    Limit from left: $x \to -2^-$, $x+2 \to 0^-$, $e^x \to e^{-2}$, denom $\to 0^-$.
    $f(x) \approx \frac{0}{0^-}$? No.
    $e^x + a = e^x - e^{-2}$.
    Let $x = -2 + \epsilon$.
    $e^{-2+\epsilon} - e^{-2} = e^{-2}(e^\epsilon - 1) \approx e^{-2}(\epsilon)$.
    Numerator $x+2 = \epsilon$.
    Ratio $\approx \frac{\epsilon}{e^{-2}\epsilon} = e^2 \approx 7.4$.
    So it's a hole? No, limit is finite?
    Wait, $f(x)$ is not defined at -2, but limit exists?
    Wait, derivative calculation $f'(x)$ assumes differentiability.
    If it's a removable singularity, the max might be elsewhere.
    But $a$ must be positive from options.
    So this case is irrelevant.

    Let's verify the derivative zero condition again.
    $f'(x) = 0 \iff a = e^x(x+1)$.
    Let $g(x) = e^x(x+1)$.
    $g'(x) = e^x(x+1) + e^x = e^x(x+2)$.
    Critical point of $g$ at $x=-2$.
    $g(-2) = e^{-2}(-1) = -1/e^2 \approx -0.135$.
    $g(-1) = 0$.
    $g(0) = 1$.
    $g(1) = 2e \approx 5.4$.
    Graph of $g(x)$:
    Decreases from $\infty$ (as $x \to -\infty$, $e^x \to 0, x+1 \to -\infty$? Wait. $e^x \to 0$, $x+1 \to -\infty$. Product $\to 0$ from below? No. $x+1$ is negative, $e^x$ positive. So $g(x) \to 0^-$?
    Let's check limit $x \to -\infty$.
    $\lim_{x \to -\infty} (x+1)e^x = \lim \frac{x+1}{e^{-x}}$. L'Hopital: $\frac{1}{-e^{-x}} = -e^x \to 0$.
    So $g(x) \to 0$ from below as $x \to -\infty$.
    Wait, if $x=-10$, $(-9)e^{-10} < 0$. Yes.
    So $g(x)$ comes from 0 (neg), goes down to min at $x=-2$ ($g(-2) = -1/e^2$), then increases.
    Crosses 0 at $x=-1$.
    Crosses 1 at $x=0$.
    Crosses $a$ at some $x$.
    Since $a \in \{0.5, 1, 1.5, 2\}$, $a > 0$.
    The equation $g(x) = a$ has exactly one solution for $x > -1$.
    Let's call this solution $x_a$.
    For $x < -1$, $g(x) < 0$ (since $x+1 < 0$ and $e^x > 0$), so no solution for $a>0$.
    Actually $g(x)$ is negative for $x < -1$.
    So critical point $x_a$ is always in $(-1, \infty)$.
    Check $f(x)$ behavior.
    $f'(x)$ has sign of $a - g(x) = a - e^x(x+1)$.
    Since $g(x)$ is increasing for $x > -2$, and $g(x)$ is increasing on $(-1, \infty)$ (subset of domain where derivative sign matters? No $-2$ is min).
    Wait, $g'(x) = e^x(x+2)$.
    $g(x)$ decreases on $(-\infty, -2)$, increases on $(-2, \infty)$.
    We are interested in roots of $g(x) = a$ where $a > 0$.
    Since min of $g(x)$ is negative, and $g(x)$ goes to $\infty$ as $x \to \infty$, there is exactly one root $x_a > -1$ such that $g(x_a) = a$.
    For $x < x_a$ (but $x > -1$), $g(x) < a$, so $a - g(x) > 0$, so $f'(x) > 0$.
    For $x > x_a$, $g(x) > a$, so $a - g(x) < 0$, so $f'(x) < 0$.
    So $x_a$ is always a global maximum for $a > 0$.
    We need $f(x_a) = 1$.
    We derived that $f(x_a)=1 \implies x_a=0$ and $a=1$.
    Since solution is unique for the system, $a=1$ is the unique solution.

    Let's quickly check the options again.

    Yes, 1 is a maximum.

    Let's solve $x+2 = e^x(1+x) + 2$? No.
    $e^x(1+x) = 2$.
    At $x=0.35$, LHS $\approx 1.91$.
    At $x=0.38$, $e^{0.38} \approx 1.46$, $1.38$. Prod $\approx 2.01$.

    Maybe $a$ could be something else? No.
    Is $x=0$ the only max?
    $f(x) = (x+2)/(e^x+1)$.
    $f'(x) = (1 - e^x(1+x))/(e^x+1)^2$.
    Numerator $h(x) = 1 - e^x - xe^x$.
    $h'(x) = -e^x - (e^x + xe^x) = -e^x(2+x)$.
    $h(x)$ increases for $x < -2$? No.
    $h'(x)$ sign depends on $-(x+2)$.
    If $x < -2$, $x+2 < 0$, $h'(x) > 0$.
    If $x > -2$, $x+2 > 0$, $h'(x) < 0$.
    So $h(x)$ increases on $(-\infty, -2)$ and decreases on $(-2, \infty)$.
    Max of $h(x)$ is at $x=-2$.
    $h(-2) = 1 - e^{-2}(-1) = 1 + 1/e^2 > 0$.
    Wait, $h(-2) = 1 - e^{-2}(-2+1)$?
    $h(x) = 1 - e^x - xe^x$.
    $h(-2) = 1 - e^{-2} - (-2)e^{-2} = 1 - e^{-2} + 2e^{-2} = 1 + e^{-2} > 0$.
    So $h(x)$ is positive at $x=-2$.
    As $x \to \infty$, $h(x) \to -\infty$ (dominated by $-xe^x$).
    As $x \to -\infty$, $h(x) \to 1$ (since $xe^x \to 0$).
    So $h(x)$ starts at 1, goes up to $1+e^{-2}$ at -2, then goes down.
    It crosses 0 exactly once.
    We found the root at $x=0$.
    $h(0) = 1 - 1 - 0 = 0$.
    So $h(x) > 0$ for $x < 0$ and $h(x) < 0$ for $x > 0$?
    Wait.
    At $x=-2$, $h(-2) > 0$.
    At $x=0$, $h(0) = 0$.
    Since $h(x)$ is decreasing on $(-2, \infty)$, $h(x)$ goes from positive to 0 at 0.
    So $h(x) > 0$ on $(-2, 0)$.
    And for $x < -2$, $h(x)$ is increasing towards $1+e^{-2}$.
    Is it positive everywhere left of -2?
    Limit at $-\infty$ is 1. Max is $1+e^{-2}$.
    So $h(x) > 0$ for all $x < 0$.
    So $f'(x) > 0$ for all $x < 0$.
    $f'(x) < 0$ for all $x > 0$.
    So $x=0$ is a global maximum.
    $f(0) = 1$.
    Correct.

    So $h(x)$ is never 0 for $x < -2$.

    It crosses 0 at $x=0$.

    For $x>0$, $h(x)<0 \implies f'(x)<0$.

    Value $f(0)=1$.
    Everything is consistent.

    So $a=1$.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

7. 一百零八塔位于宁夏回族自治区青铜峡市，以其独特的建筑格局和深厚的历史文化闻名遐迩。该塔群共有108座塔，依山势自上而下排成12行，将第i行中塔的座数记为a_i(i=1,2,...,12)，其中a_1=1, a_2=a_3=3, a_4=a_5=5，且a_6, a_7, ..., a_{12}是一个首项为7，公差为2的等差数列。将a_1, a_2, ..., a_{12}分为6组，每组2个数，使得每组的2个数之和可构成一个项数为6且公差为d(d>0)的等差数列，则d = （ ）
A. 2
B. 4
C. 6
D. 8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**步骤一：确定数列 $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_{12}$ 的具体取值**
根据题目条件：
- $a_1 = 1$
- $a_2 = a_3 = 3$
- $a_4 = a_5 = 5$
- $a_6, a_7, \dots, a_{12}$ 是首项为7，公差为2的等差数列，共7项：$7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19$

将全部项列出：$1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19$。
验证总数：$1 + (3+3) + (5+5) + (7+9+11+13+15+17+19) = 1 + 6 + 10 + 91 = 108$，与题目中“共有108座塔”相符。

**步骤二：建立等差数列求和关系**
将这12个数分成6组，每组2个数。设这6个组数的和分别为 $S_1, S_2, S_3, S_4, S_5, S_6$（按从小到大的顺序排列）。
根据题意，$S_1, S_2, \dots, S_6$ 构成公差为 $d (d>0)$ 的等差数列。
所有组数之和等于原数列总和，即：
$$S_1 + S_2 + \dots + S_6 = 108$$
利用等差数列求和公式 $S_{sum} = \frac{n}{2}[2a_1 + (n-1)d]$，代入 $n=6$：
$$\frac{6}{2}[2S_1 + 5d] = 108 \Rightarrow 3(2S_1 + 5d) = 108 \Rightarrow 2S_1 + 5d = 36$$

**步骤三：利用奇偶性与正数条件分析**
原数列中的每一个数 $a_i$ 都是**奇数**。两个奇数相加的结果必然是**偶数**。因此，$S_1, S_2, \dots, S_6$ 都是偶数。特别地，首项 $S_1$ 必须是偶数。
由 $2S_1 = 36 - 5d$ 可知，右边 $36 - 5d$ 必须为偶数且大于0（因为 $S_1>0$）。
- 若 $36 - 5d$ 为偶数，则 $5d$ 必须为偶数，故 **$d$ 必须是偶数**。
- 若 $S_1 > 0$，则 $2S_1 > 0 \Rightarrow 36 - 5d > 0 \Rightarrow d < 7.2$。

结合选项 A(2)、B(4)、C(6)、D(8) 进行代入检验：
- 当 $d=2$ 时，$2S_1 = 36 - 10 = 26 \Rightarrow S_1 = 13$（奇数，不符合“和为偶数”的条件，排除）
- 当 $d=4$ 时，$2S_1 = 36 - 20 = 16 \Rightarrow S_1 = 8$（偶数，符合）
- 当 $d=6$ 时，$2S_1 = 36 - 30 = 6 \Rightarrow S_1 = 3$（奇数，排除）
- 当 $d=8$ 时，$2S_1 = 36 - 40 = -4 \Rightarrow S_1 = -2$（负数，不符合座数之和为正，排除）

**步骤四：验证 $d=4$ 的可行性**
当 $d=4$ 时，六组之和为 $8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28$。
我们可以尝试配对原数列中的数以得到这些和：
- $8 = 1 + 7$
- $12 = 3 + 9$
- $16 = 5 + 11$
- $20 = 3 + 17$
- $24 = 5 + 19$
- $28 = 13 + 15$
所有12个数恰好各用一次，配对成功。因此 $d=4$ 是唯一解。

最终答案为 $\boxed{B}$。

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

8. 设U={(x_1, x_2, x_3)|x_i \in \{-2,-1,1,2\}, i=1,2,3}\}为空间中64个点构成的集合，点P(1,1,1)，记样本空间\Omega = C_U^P，从\Omega中随机取一个点，定义随机变量X如下：对\Omega中的每个点A(x_1, x_2, x_3)，令X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3，则X的数学期望值为（ ）
A. -\frac{1}{21}
B. -\frac{1}{63}
C. 0
D. \frac{1}{7}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
为了解决本题，我们需要逐步分析题目给出的集合定义、样本空间以及随机变量的性质。

**1. 分析集合 $U$**
集合 $U$ 定义为 $U = \{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \mid x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}, i=1,2,3\}$。
*   每个坐标 $x_i$ 的取值有 4 种可能：$\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$。
*   集合 $U$ 中点的总数量（样本空间的潜在大小）为 $4 \times 4 \times 4 = 64$。
*   注意集合中不包含 0。
*   观察坐标值集合 $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$，它是关于 0 对称的（即对于每个 $k$，$-k$ 也在此集合中）。因此，集合 $U$ 关于原点对称。

**2. 分析样本空间 $\Omega$**
题目记样本空间为 $\Omega = C_U^P$，其中 $P(1, 1, 1)$ 是一个给定的点。
*   首先检查点 $P$ 是否属于 $U$。由于 $1 \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$，所以 $P(1, 1, 1) \in U$。
*   符号 $C_U^P$ 在此语境下通常表示集合 $U$ 中除去点 $P$ 后的剩余部分（即 $U$ 中关于 $P$ 的补集），即 $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$。
*   因此，$\Omega$ 中点的数量为 $|U| - 1 = 64 - 1 = 63$。

**3. 分析随机变量 $X$**
对于 $\Omega$ 中的每个点 $A(x_1, x_2, x_3)$，随机变量定义为 $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$。
我们需要求 $X$ 的数学期望值 $E[X]$。根据定义：
$$E[X] = \frac{\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A)}{|\Omega|}$$

**4. 计算求和 $\sum X(A)$**
首先计算集合 $U$ 中所有点的坐标和的总和。
$$S_U = \sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$$
由于集合 $U$ 的对称性，对于集合中的每一个点 $(x_1, x_2, x_3)$，必定存在另一个点 $(-x_1, -x_2, -x_3)$ 也在集合中。这两个点的坐标和分别为 $S$ 和 $-S$，相加为 0。
因此，集合 $U$ 中所有点的坐标和的总和为 0。
$$S_U = 0$$

现在，我们需要计算 $\Omega$ 中所有点的坐标和的总和。由于 $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$，我们只需从总和中减去点 $P$ 的坐标和。
点 $P$ 的坐标为 $(1, 1, 1)$，其坐标和为：
$$X(P) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$$
因此，$\Omega$ 中点的坐标和总和为：
$$S_\Omega = S_U - X(P) = 0 - 3 = -3$$

**5. 计算期望值**
样本空间 $\Omega$ 的总点数为 63。
$$E[X] = \frac{S_\Omega}{|\Omega|} = \frac{-3}{63}$$
化简分数：
$$E[X] = -\frac{1}{21}$$

**结论**
随机变量 $X$ 的数学期望值为 $-\frac{1}{21}$。

对比选项：
A. $-\frac{1}{21}$
B. $-\frac{1}{63}$
C. $0$
D. $\frac{1}{7}$

正确答案为 A。

\boxed{A}

        6.  Sum of $X(A)$ for all $A \in U$.

        8.  Size of $\Omega$.
            *   $|\Omega| = |U| - 1 = 64 - 1 = 63$.
        9.  Calculate Expectation.
            *   $E[X] = \frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Size}} = \frac{-3}{63} = -\frac{1}{21}$.
        10. Match with options. Option A is $-\frac{1}{21}$.

    *   **Step-by-step derivation for the final output:**
        1.  **Analyze Set $U$**: $U = \{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \mid x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}\}$.
        2.  **Size of $U$**: $4 \times 4 \times 4 = 64$.
        3.  **Analyze Sample Space $\Omega$**: The notation $\Omega = C_U^P$ is interpreted as the set $U$ excluding the point $P(1, 1, 1)$. Thus, $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$. The size $|\Omega| = 64 - 1 = 63$. (Reasoning: $P \in U$, removing it is the only logical operation fitting the options and context).
        4.  **Analyze Random Variable $X$**: For a point $A(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in \Omega$, $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$.
        5.  **Calculate Total Sum of $X(A)$ for $A \in U$**:
            *   $\sum_{A \in U} X(A) = \sum_{A \in U} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$.
            *   Due to symmetry of the coordinate set $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$ (sum is 0), the sum of coordinates over the full Cartesian product is 0.
            *   Detailed check: $\sum_{x_1, x_2, x_3} x_1 = (\sum x_1)(\sum 1)(\sum 1) = (0)(4)(4) = 0$. Same for $x_2, x_3$. Total sum = 0.
        6.  **Calculate Sum of $X(A)$ for $A \in \Omega$**:
            *   Sum$_\Omega$ = Sum$_U$ - $X(P)$.
            *   $X(P) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$.
            *   Sum$_\Omega$ = $0 - 3 = -3$.
        7.  **Calculate Expected Value $E[X]$**:
            *   $E[X] = \frac{\text{Sum}_\Omega}{|\Omega|} = \frac{-3}{63}$.
            *   Simplify fraction: $-3/63 = -1/21$.
        8.  **Select Option**: A.

    *   **Alternative Check**: What if $\Omega = U$?
        *   $E[X] = 0/64 = 0$. Option C.

The symmetry of the full set yields a sum of 0. Removing $(1,1,1)$ which has a sum of 3 leaves a total sum of -3. Dividing by the remaining 63 points gives -1/21.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

9. 设z = 3 + 2i，则（ ）
A. \bar{z} = 3 - 2i
B. |z| = 5
C. z^2 = 5 + 12i
D. \frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
逐步分析各选项：

已知 $z = 3 + 2i$。

**A. 验证共轭复数**
复数 $z = a + bi$ 的共轭复数为 $\bar{z} = a - bi$。
因此，$\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$。与选项A一致，**正确**。

**B. 验证复数模**
复数的模长公式为 $|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$。
计算得：$|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13}$。
$\sqrt{13} \neq 5$，故**错误**。

**C. 验证复数平方**
$z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \times 3 \times 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4i^2$。
由于 $i^2 = -1$，所以 $z^2 = 9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$。
与选项C一致，**正确**。

**D. 验证分式是否为实数**
代入 $z = 3 + 2i$：
$$\frac{z+3}{z-i} = \frac{(3+2i)+3}{(3+2i)-i} = \frac{6+2i}{3+i}$$
分子分母同乘分母的共轭复数 $3-i$：
$$\frac{(6+2i)(3-i)}{(3+i)(3-i)} = \frac{18 - 6i + 6i - 2i^2}{9 - i^2} = \frac{18 + 2}{9 + 1} = \frac{20}{10} = 2$$
结果 $2$ 是一个实数，即 $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$。故**正确**。

综上，A、C、D 均为正确选项。

\boxed{ACD}

Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

10. 在空间中，A、B为两个定点，动点C到直线AB的距离为2，动点D到直线AB的距离为1。若二面角C - AB - D为60°，则（ ）
A. \angle CAD \ge 60^\circ
B. CD \ge \sqrt{3}
C. 当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD
D. 当AB \perp平面ACD时，AC \perp AD

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
To analyze the problem, we establish a coordinate system with line $AB$ as the $z$-axis and point $A$ at the origin $(0,0,0)$.

1.  **Coordinates of Moving Points:**
    *   Point $C$ has a distance of 2 from the $z$-axis, so $C = (2 \cos \alpha, 2 \sin \alpha, z_C)$.
    *   Point $D$ has a distance of 1 from the $z$-axis, so $D = (\cos \beta, \sin \beta, z_D)$.

2.  **Dihedral Angle Constraint:**
    The dihedral angle between planes $(ABC)$ and $(ABD)$ is $60^\circ$. The normal vectors to these planes are $\vec{n}_1 = (-2 \sin \alpha, 2 \cos \alpha, 0)$ and $\vec{n}_2 = (-\sin \beta, \cos \beta, 0)$.
    The cosine of the angle between the planes is:
    $$\cos 60^\circ = \left| \frac{\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2}{|\vec{n}_1||\vec{n}_2|} \right| = \left| \frac{2 \cos(\alpha - \beta)}{2 \cdot 1} \right| = |\cos(\alpha - \beta)| = \frac{1}{2}$$
    This implies $\alpha - \beta = \pm 60^\circ$ or $\pm 120^\circ$.

3.  **Analysis of Option B ($CD \ge \sqrt{3}$):**
    The squared distance between $C$ and $D$ is:
    $$CD^2 = (2 \cos \alpha - \cos \beta)^2 + (2 \sin \alpha - \sin \beta)^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2$$
    $$CD^2 = 4 \cos^2 \alpha - 4 \cos \alpha \cos \beta + \cos^2 \beta + 4 \sin^2 \alpha - 4 \sin \alpha \sin \beta + \sin^2 \beta + (z_C - z_D)^2$$
    $$CD^2 = 5 - 4 \cos(\alpha - \beta) + (z_C - z_D)^2$$
    Substituting $\cos(\alpha - \beta) = \pm \frac{1}{2}$:
    *   If $\cos(\alpha - \beta) = \frac{1}{2}$, $CD^2 = 5 - 2 + (z_C - z_D)^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
    *   If $\cos(\alpha - \beta) = -\frac{1}{2}$, $CD^2 = 5 + 2 + (z_C - z_D)^2 = 7 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 7$.
    In both cases, $CD^2 \ge 3$, which means $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$. This statement is always true.

4.  **Evaluation of Other Options:**
    *   **Option A ($\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$):** If $z_C$ and $z_D$ are large and equal, the vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ become nearly parallel to the $z$-axis, making the angle approach $0^\circ$. This statement is false.
    *   **Option C (If $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$):** $AB \perp CD$ implies $z_C = z_D$. Analysis shows this perpendicularity only holds if $\cos(\alpha - \beta) = \frac{1}{2}$ ($60^\circ$ between projections). If the angle between projections is $120^\circ$, this implication fails.
    *   **Option D (If $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$):** $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$ implies $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$, forcing $C$ and $D$ into the $xy$-plane. In this configuration, $\angle CAD$ must be the dihedral angle ($60^\circ$ or $120^\circ$), so $AC$ is never perpendicular to $AD$.

The only universally true statement is B.

\boxed{B}

            *   The dihedral angle is the angle between the lines $C P_C$ and $D P_D$?

            *   Wait, if I pick $C'$ on one side and $D'$ on the "opposite" side of the intersection?
            *   Yes.

            *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D". The vertices are listed. This might imply orientation?
            *   Usually, in such geometry problems, "angle C-AB-D" means the angle between the planes containing the triangles (or half-planes) defined by the points.
            *   If it's just the angle between planes, it's $60$.
            *   If $C'$ and $D'$ are on the "same side" of the edge in terms of angular position?
            *   Let's assume the worst case for distance (max distance) or check if min distance is bounded.
            *   We found min distance is $\sqrt{3}$ if angle is $60^\circ$.

            *   If the dihedral angle is $60^\circ$, the planes intersect at $60^\circ$.
            *   The "interior" angle is usually considered.
            *   However, $C$ and $D$ are points. They are not rays.
            *   If $C$ is at $(2, 0, 0)$ and $D$ is at $(-1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$, the angle between the plane $y=0$ (containing $C$ and $z$-axis) and plane passing through $z$-axis and $D$.
            *   Normal of plane $C$ is $(0, 1, 0)$.

            *   $\phi = 120^\circ$.

            *   Usually, if not specified, we assume the configuration allows for the minimal distance, or the question implies a specific geometry where B must hold.
            *   Given B holds for $60^\circ$ and $120^\circ$, it is likely correct.
            *   Let's double check calculation.
            *   $CD^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
            *   This relied on $C'$ and $D'$ distance being $\sqrt{3}$.
            *   This relied on angle between $C'$ and $D'$ being $60^\circ$.
            *   If angle is $120^\circ$, distance is $\sqrt{7} > \sqrt{3}$.
            *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$ is robust.
            *   Let's check if angle could be something else?
            *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are on the same plane? No, angle is 60.
            *   Maybe angle is $60$ but $C'$ and $D'$ are arranged differently?
            *   Distance between points on two rays originating from origin with angle $\alpha$ and distances $r_1, r_2$ is $\sqrt{r_1^2 + r_2^2 - 2r_1 r_2 \cos \alpha}$.
            *   Here $r_1 = 2, r_2 = 1$.
            *   If angle $\alpha = 60^\circ$, dist = $\sqrt{4+1-2} = \sqrt{3}$.
            *   If angle $\alpha = 120^\circ$, dist = $\sqrt{4+1+2} = \sqrt{7}$.
            *   If angle $\alpha = 180^\circ$ (impossible for 60 dihedral), dist = 3.
            *   If angle $\alpha = 0$ (impossible), dist = 1.
            *   Is it possible for angle to be $60^\circ$ but the distance to be smaller? No.
            *   Is it possible for the "angle between planes" to be $60^\circ$ but the angle between projections to be something else?
            *   No, the angle between planes is the angle between the lines of intersection with a plane perpendicular to the edge.
            *   So the angle between vectors $\vec{PC'}$ and $\vec{PD'}$ must be $60^\circ$ or $120^\circ$ (supplementary).
            *   Wait, could it be that $C'$ and $D'$ are not in the same perpendicular plane?
            *   $C'$ is projection of $C$ on plane $\perp AB$. $D'$ is projection of $D$ on plane $\perp AB$.

            *   No.
            *   Let's be precise.

            *   Similarly $\vec{n}_D$ is perpendicular to $\vec{v}_D = (x_D, y_D)$.

            *   So the angle between the position vectors of the projections is exactly the dihedral angle (or $180 - \text{dihedral}$).
            *   So the angle between $\vec{OC'}$ and $\vec{OD'}$ is $60^\circ$ (or $120^\circ$).
            *   So the distance $C'D'$ is $\sqrt{3}$ or $\sqrt{7}$.
            *   In both cases, $C'D' \ge \sqrt{3}$.
            *   Then $CD^2 = C'D'^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
            *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.

    *   **Analyze Option C: When $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$**
        *   If $AB \perp CD$, what does that imply?

        *   (Assuming angle is 60. If 120, it's -1).

    *   **Analyze Option D: When $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$**

    *   **Re-evaluating Option C and B.**
        *   I found B is always true ($CD \ge \sqrt{3}$).

        *   Angle $\alpha$ between planes satisfies $\cos \alpha = \frac{\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2}{|\vec{n}_1| |\vec{n}_2|}$.
        *   $\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2 = (-y_C)(-y_D) + (x_C)(x_D) = x_C x_D + y_C y_D$.
        *   $|\vec{n}_1| = \sqrt{y_C^2 + x_C^2} = \sqrt{x_C^2 + y_C^2} = \text{dist}(C, AB) = 2$.
        *   $|\vec{n}_2| = \sqrt{y_D^2 + x_D^2} = \text{dist}(D, AB) = 1$.
        *   So $\cos \alpha = \frac{x_C x_D + y_C y_D}{2 \cdot 1}$.
        *   We are given dihedral angle is $60^\circ$. So $\alpha = 60^\circ$ (or $120^\circ$).
        *   So $\frac{x_C x_D + y_C y_D}{2} = \cos 60^\circ = 1/2$ (or $\cos 120^\circ = -1/2$).
        *   So $x_C x_D + y_C y_D = 1$ (or $-1$).

        *   However, B ($CD \ge \sqrt{3}$) is true in this case too ($CD = \sqrt{(-1.5)^2 + (-\sqrt{3}/2)^2} = \sqrt{2.25 + 0.75} = \sqrt{3}$).
        *   Wait, distance calculation:
        *   $C'=(2,0)$, $D'=(-0.5, -0.866)$.
        *   $CD' = \sqrt{(2 - (-0.5))^2 + (0 - (-0.866))^2} = \sqrt{2.5^2 + 0.866^2} = \sqrt{6.25 + 0.75} = \sqrt{7}$.
        *   Wait, distance is $\sqrt{7}$.
        *   $\sqrt{7} > \sqrt{3}$.
        *   So B holds.
        *   But in the previous case (angle 60 between vectors), distance was $\sqrt{3}$.
        *   So min distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
        *   So B is always true.

    *   **Let's double check Option A again.**
        *   $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
        *   Counter example: $C=(2,0,100)$, $D=(0.5, \sqrt{3}/2, 100)$.

        *   Angle between planes is 60.

    *   **Let's double check Option D again.**
        *   "When $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$".
        *   Condition $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$ means $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
        *   Since $A$ is on $AB$, this means $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$.
        *   So $C$ and $D$ must be in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
        *   So $z_C = 0, z_D = 0$ (assuming $A$ is origin).
        *   $C$ is distance 2 from $A$ in $xy$-plane.
        *   $D$ is distance 1 from $A$ in $xy$-plane.
        *   Angle between planes is 60.
        *   This means angle between $AC$ and $AD$ is 60?
        *   Plane $ABC$ contains $AB$ and $AC$. Since $AC \perp AB$, angle between plane $ABC$ and plane perpendicular to $AB$ is 90?
        *   No.
        *   Plane $ABC$ is vertical (contains $z$-axis). $AC$ is horizontal.
        *   Plane $ABD$ is vertical. $AD$ is horizontal.
        *   The angle between vertical planes is the angle between their horizontal traces ($AC$ and $AD$).
        *   So $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
        *   We need to check if $AC \perp AD$.
        *   Is $60^\circ = 90^\circ$? No.
        *   So $AC \not\perp AD$.
        *   So D is false.
        *   Wait, is it possible for $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$ to hold in a different configuration?
        *   $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$ implies $AB$ is normal to the plane.
        *   So $AB$ is perpendicular to any vector in plane $ACD$.
        *   Vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ are in the plane.
        *   So $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{AC} = 0$ and $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0$.
        *   Since $\vec{AB}$ is along $z$-axis, this implies $z_C = z_A$ and $z_D = z_A$.
        *   So $C$ and $D$ must be at same height as $A$.
        *   So my analysis holds.
        *   D is false.

    *   **Conclusion so far:**
        *   A: False.
        *   B: True.
        *   C: Conditionally True (True if angle between projections is 60, False if 120). But B is always True.
        *   D: False.

    *   **Wait, let me rethink C.**
        *   Maybe I'm misinterpreting "Dihedral angle C-AB-D".
        *   In many contexts (especially Chinese math problems), "Dihedral angle C-AB-D" is defined as the angle between the planes.
        *   However, sometimes it refers to the angle between the vectors $AC$ and $AD$ projected? No.

        *   But without vector notation, it's ambiguous.
        *   However, usually "angle 60" implies the acute angle.
        *   If it's acute angle, both configurations (vectors angle 60 or 120) correspond to planes at 60.
        *   So C is not strictly true.
        *   B seems robustly true.

    *   **Let's check B again.**
        *   Is it possible for $CD < \sqrt{3}$?
        *   $CD^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$ assuming angle between projections is 60.
        *   If angle is 120, $CD^2 = 7 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 7$.
        *   Is there any case where angle is not 60 or 120?
        *   No, planes intersect at 60.
        *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$ is solid.
        *   Is $\sqrt{3}$ achievable?
        *   Yes, if $z_C = z_D$ and angle between projections is 60.
        *   Example: $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,1)$.
        *   $C=(2,0,0)$. Dist 2.
        *   $D=(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$. Dist 1.
        *   Plane $ABC$: $y=0$.
        *   Plane $ABD$: passes through $z$-axis and $(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   Equation $-\sqrt{3}x + y = 0$?
        *   Normal $(-\sqrt{3}, 1, 0)$.
        *   Normal of $ABC$ is $(0,1,0)$.
        *   Dot product $1$.
        *   Magnitudes $\sqrt{3}$ and $1$.
        *   $\cos \theta = 1/\sqrt{3} \approx 0.577$.
        *   Wait. Angle is $\arccos(1/\sqrt{3}) \approx 54.7^\circ$.
        *   This is not 60.
        *   Why?
        *   Ah. My coordinate choice for $D$ was based on angle 60 between projections.
        *   $D' = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2)$.
        *   This vector is at $60^\circ$ from x-axis.
        *   $C' = (2, 0)$. Along x-axis.
        *   So angle between projections is 60.
        *   Does this imply dihedral angle is 60?
        *   Let's re-evaluate the relation between angle of projections and dihedral angle.
        *   Plane 1 contains $z$-axis and $C(2,0,z_C)$.
        *   Vector in plane 1: $\vec{v}_1 = (2, 0, z_C)$.
        *   Vector along axis: $\vec{k} = (0,0,1)$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n}_1 = \vec{v}_1 \times \vec{k} = (-z_C \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 1, 0 \cdot 0 - 2 \cdot 1, 2 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0)$?
        *   Wait, cross product formula:
            *   $\vec{u} \times \vec{v} = (u_y v_z - u_z v_y, u_z v_x - u_x v_z, u_x v_y - u_y v_x)$.
            *   $\vec{v}_1 = (2, 0, z_C)$. $\vec{k} = (0, 0, 1)$.
            *   $x: 0 \cdot 1 - z_C \cdot 0 = 0$.
            *   $y: z_C \cdot 0 - 2 \cdot 1 = -2$.
            *   $z: 2 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0 = 0$.
            *   So $\vec{n}_1 = (0, -2, 0)$.
        *   Wait, this normal depends on $z_C$?
        *   No, it's $(0, -2, 0)$. Constant!
        *   Wait, if $C$ moves in $z$, the plane $(ABC)$ changes?
        *   $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,1)$, $C=(2,0, z_C)$.
        *   Points $A, B, C$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AC} = (2, 0, z_C)$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AC} = (0,0,1) \times (2,0,z_C) = (-2, 0, 0)$.
        *   Wait, previously I calculated $(0, -2, 0)$. Let's recheck.
        *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$. $\vec{AC} = (2,0,z_C)$.
        *   $i(0 \cdot z_C - 1 \cdot 0) - j(0 \cdot z_C - 1 \cdot 2) + k(0 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 2)$.
        *   $i(0) - j(-2) + k(0) = (0, 2, 0)$.
        *   So normal is $(0, 1, 0)$.
        *   This is perpendicular to x-z plane? No, y-axis.
        *   Plane $ABC$ is the x-z plane ($y=0$).
        *   Correct.
        *   Now Plane $ABD$.
        *   $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,1)$, $D=(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, z_D)$.
        *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$.
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, z_D)$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n}_2 = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (0,0,1) \times (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, z_D)$.
        *   $x: 0 \cdot z_D - 1 \cdot (\sqrt{3}/2) = -\sqrt{3}/2$.
        *   $y: 1 \cdot (1/2) - 0 \cdot z_D = 1/2$.
        *   $z: 0 \cdot (\sqrt{3}/2) - 0 \cdot (1/2) = 0$.
        *   So $\vec{n}_2 = (-\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Angle between $\vec{n}_1 = (0, 1, 0)$ and $\vec{n}_2 = (-\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Dot product: $0(-\sqrt{3}/2) + 1(1/2) + 0 = 1/2$.
        *   Magnitudes: $|\vec{n}_1| = 1$. $|\vec{n}_2| = \sqrt{3/4 + 1/4} = 1$.
        *   $\cos \theta = 1/2$.
        *   $\theta = 60^\circ$.
        *   So yes, if projections are at 60 degrees, dihedral angle is 60.
        *   And my previous calculation of distance was correct.
        *   So B is correct.

    *   **Let's check C again with this specific example.**
        *   $C=(2,0,0)$, $D=(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $z_C = 0, z_D = 0$. So $AB \perp CD$ holds.
        *   Is $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$?
        *   $\vec{CD} = D - C = (-1.5, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ normal $\vec{n}_2 = (-\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Are they parallel?
        *   $\vec{CD} = k \vec{n}_2$?
        *   $-1.5 = k (-\sqrt{3}/2) \implies k = 1.5 \cdot 2 / \sqrt{3} = 3/\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   Check y: $\sqrt{3}/2 = \sqrt{3} (1/2)$. Yes.
        *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal.
        *   So $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
        *   So C is true for this example.

    *   **Let's check the counter-example for C (angle 120).**
        *   $C=(2,0,0)$. $D=(-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = (-2.5, -\sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ normal $\vec{n}_2$.
        *   $D=(-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{AD} = (-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{n}_2 = (0,0,1) \times (-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $x: 0 - (-\sqrt{3}/2) = \sqrt{3}/2$.
        *   $y: -(-1/2) - 0 = 1/2$.
        *   $\vec{n}_2 = (\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Is $\vec{CD}$ parallel to $\vec{n}_2$?
        *   $(-2.5, -\sqrt{3}/2)$ vs $(\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2)$.
        *   Ratio x: $-2.5 / (\sqrt{3}/2) = -5/\sqrt{3} \approx -2.88$.
        *   Ratio y: $(-\sqrt{3}/2) / (1/2) = -\sqrt{3} \approx -1.73$.
        *   Not parallel.
        *   So $CD \not\perp$ plane $ABD$.
        *   But is this configuration valid?
        *   Dihedral angle check.
        *   $\vec{n}_1 = (0, 1, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{n}_2 = (\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Dot product $1/2$.
        *   Angle 60.
        *   Wait.
        *   If $\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2 = 1/2$, angle is 60.
        *   So this configuration *does* have dihedral angle 60.
        *   But C fails.
        *   Why?
        *   Because $D$ is at angle 240 (or -120) relative to $C$ (angle 0).
        *   Angle between vectors is 120.
        *   But angle between normals is 60.
        *   Wait, if angle between normals is 60, then angle between planes is 60.
        *   So yes, valid.
        *   So C is not always true.

    *   **Why is B always true?**
        *   Because distance depends on angle between projections.
        *   Distance squared $d^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1) \cos(\text{angle between projections})$.
        *   We established that angle between projections can be 60 or 120 (or 180-60).
        *   $\cos(60) = 1/2 \implies d^2 = 4+1-2 = 3$.
        *   $\cos(120) = -1/2 \implies d^2 = 4+1+2 = 7$.
        *   Wait, is it possible to have angle between projections be something else?
        *   Normal vector angle $\theta_n = 60$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n}_1$ is rotated 90 from projection vector $\vec{v}_1$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n}_2$ is rotated 90 from projection vector $\vec{v}_2$.
        *   If $\vec{v}_1$ is at angle 0. $\vec{n}_1$ is at 90.
        *   If angle between normals is 60, then $\vec{n}_2$ is at 90+60=150 or 90-60=30.
        *   So angle of $\vec{n}_2$ is $\pm 60$ relative to $\vec{n}_1$.
        *   Angle of $\vec{v}_2$ is angle of $\vec{n}_2$ minus 90.
        *   So angle of $\vec{v}_2$ relative to $\vec{v}_1$ is $(90 \pm 60) - 90 = \pm 60$.
        *   So angle between projections is always $60^\circ$ (or $-60^\circ$).
        *   Wait, what about 120?
        *   If angle between normals is 60, the angle between vectors is 60.
        *   What if angle between normals is 120?
        *   Angle between planes is 60 (since $180-120=60$).
        *   If angle between normals is 120.
        *   $\vec{n}_1$ at 90. $\vec{n}_2$ at $90+120=210$ or $90-120=-30$.
        *   Angle of $\vec{v}_2$ is $210-90=120$ or $-30-90=-120$.
        *   So angle between projections is 120.
        *   So yes, angle between projections is either 60 or 120.
        *   Distance squared is 3 or 7.
        *   In both cases $\ge 3$.
        *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
        *   So B is always true.

    *   **Is there any catch?**
        *   "A, B are fixed points".
        *   Maybe $A$ is not on the line connecting the projections?
        *   No, $A$ is on line $AB$.
        *   $P_C$ is projection of $C$ on line $AB$.
        *   $P_D$ is projection of $D$ on line $AB$.
        *   Distance $CD^2 = C'D'^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2$.
        *   $C'$ is proj of $C$ on plane $\perp AB$ at some point?
        *   Actually, let's use vector decomposition.
        *   Let $\vec{u}$ be unit vector along $AB$.
        *   $C = C_{\parallel} + C_{\perp}$. $D = D_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}$.
        *   $C_{\perp} \perp \vec{u}$, $|C_{\perp}| = 2$.
        *   $D_{\perp} \perp \vec{u}$, $|D_{\perp}| = 1$.
        *   $C_{\parallel} = k_1 \vec{u}$, $D_{\parallel} = k_2 \vec{u}$.
        *   Plane $(ABC)$ contains $\vec{u}$ and $C$. So it contains $\vec{u}$ and $C_{\perp}$ (since $C = C_{\parallel} + C_{\perp}$ and $C_{\parallel}$ is parallel to $\vec{u}$).
        *   Actually, plane is spanned by $\vec{u}$ and $C_{\perp}$.
        *   Similarly plane $(ABD)$ is spanned by $\vec{u}$ and $D_{\perp}$.
        *   Dihedral angle is angle between plane spanned by $(\vec{u}, C_{\perp})$ and plane spanned by $(\vec{u}, D_{\perp})$.
        *   The angle between these planes is the angle between $C_{\perp}$ and $D_{\perp}$.
        *   Let $\theta$ be angle between $C_{\perp}$ and $D_{\perp}$.
        *   Then dihedral angle is $\theta$ (or $180-\theta$).
        *   Given as 60. So $\theta = 60^\circ$ or $120^\circ$.
        *   Now calculate $|\vec{CD}|^2 = |(D_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}) - (C_{\parallel} + C_{\perp})|^2$.
        *   $= |(D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel}) + (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp})|^2$.
        *   Since parallel and perpendicular parts are orthogonal:
        *   $= |D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel}|^2 + |D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}|^2$.
        *   $|D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel}|^2 = (k_2 - k_1)^2$ (scalar squared).
        *   $|D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}|^2 = |D_{\perp}|^2 + |C_{\perp}|^2 - 2 D_{\perp} \cdot C_{\perp}$.
        *   $= 1^2 + 2^2 - 2(1)(2) \cos \theta$.
        *   $= 5 - 4 \cos \theta$.
        *   If $\theta = 60^\circ$, $\cos \theta = 1/2$, term is $5 - 2 = 3$.
        *   If $\theta = 120^\circ$, $\cos \theta = -1/2$, term is $5 + 2 = 7$.
        *   So perpendicular distance squared is 3 or 7.
        *   Total distance squared $CD^2 = (k_2 - k_1)^2 + 3$ (or 7).
        *   $(k_2 - k_1)^2 \ge 0$.
        *   So $CD^2 \ge 3$.
        *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
        *   This confirms B is strictly true.

    *   **Let's check C again with this vector notation.**
        *   $AB \perp CD \iff \vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = 0$.
        *   $\vec{AB}$ is parallel to $\vec{u}$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = (D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel}) + (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp})$.
        *   $\vec{u} \cdot (D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel})$ is non-zero component.
        *   $\vec{u} \cdot (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) = 0$.
        *   So $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = |\vec{AB}| (k_2 - k_1)$.
        *   For this to be 0, we need $k_2 = k_1$.
        *   i.e., $D_{\parallel} = C_{\parallel}$.
        *   This means $P_D = P_C$. $D$ and $C$ project to the same point on line $AB$.
        *   So $z_D = z_C$.
        *   Now check if $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ is spanned by $\vec{u}$ and $D_{\perp}$ (since $D = D_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}$ and $D_{\parallel}$ is along $\vec{u}$).
        *   Actually plane $ABD$ contains $\vec{u}$ and vector $\vec{AD} = A_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}$?
        *   Wait, $A$ is a point. Let's set $A$ as origin for vectors?
        *   Let $A$ be origin.
        *   $\vec{A} = 0$.
        *   $C$ vector $\vec{C}$. $D$ vector $\vec{D}$.
        *   $\vec{C} = C_{\parallel} + C_{\perp}$. $C_{\parallel} = k_1 \vec{u}$.
        *   $\vec{D} = D_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}$. $D_{\parallel} = k_2 \vec{u}$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains $\vec{u}$ and $\vec{D}$.
        *   Actually contains $\vec{u}$ (since $B$ is on line) and $\vec{D}$.
        *   Since $\vec{D} = D_{\parallel} + D_{\perp}$ and $D_{\parallel}$ is parallel to $\vec{u}$, the plane is spanned by $\vec{u}$ and $D_{\perp}$.
        *   Normal to plane $ABD$ is $\vec{n} = \vec{u} \times D_{\perp}$.
        *   We need $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{n}$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = \vec{D} - \vec{C} = (D_{\parallel} - C_{\parallel}) + (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp})$.
        *   Condition $AB \perp CD \implies D_{\parallel} = C_{\parallel}$.
        *   So $\vec{CD} = D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}$.
        *   We need $(D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \perp (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   Dot product: $(D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}) = 0$.
        *   Scalar triple product $[D_{\perp}, C_{\perp}, \vec{u} \times D_{\perp}]$? No.
        *   Distribute: $D_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}) - C_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   First term: $D_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}) = 0$ (dot product with cross product of self).
        *   Second term: $- C_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   $= - [\vec{u}, D_{\perp}, C_{\perp}]$ (scalar triple product).
        *   $= - \det(\vec{u}, D_{\perp}, C_{\perp})$.
        *   We need this to be 0.
        *   Since $\vec{u}$ is unit vector (say $z$), and $D_{\perp}, C_{\perp}$ are in $xy$-plane.
        *   Determinant of matrix with rows $(0,0,1)$, $(x_D, y_D, 0)$, $(x_C, y_C, 0)$.
        *   $= 1 \cdot (x_D y_C - y_D x_C)$.
        *   $= x_D y_C - y_D x_C$.
        *   This is the 2D cross product (magnitude of cross product) of projection vectors.
        *   It is 0 if and only if $D_{\perp}$ and $C_{\perp}$ are collinear (parallel).
        *   i.e., angle between $C_{\perp}$ and $D_{\perp}$ is 0 or 180.
        *   But we know angle is 60 or 120.
        *   So they are never collinear.
        *   So the dot product is never 0.
        *   So $CD$ is never perpendicular to normal of plane $ABD$.
        *   Wait, did I mess up?
        *   Let's re-evaluate "Is $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$?"
        *   We found earlier that in the specific example ($C=(2,0,0), D=(0.5, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$), $CD$ was parallel to normal.
        *   Let's check the cross product term.
        *   $C_{\perp} = (2, 0)$. $D_{\perp} = (0.5, \sqrt{3}/2)$.
        *   $x_D y_C - y_D x_C = (0.5)(0) - (\sqrt{3}/2)(2) = -\sqrt{3} \ne 0$.
        *   Wait, my derivation says it should be non-zero.
        *   But my check said it was parallel.
        *   Let's re-check the check.
        *   $C=(2,0,0)$. $D=(0.5, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = (-1.5, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ normal $\vec{n}_2 = (-\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
        *   Is $\vec{CD}$ parallel to $\vec{n}_2$?
        *   $\vec{CD} = (-1.5, 0.866, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{n}_2 = (-0.866, 0.5, 0)$.
        *   Ratio x: $-1.5 / -0.866 = 1.732 = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   Ratio y: $0.866 / 0.5 = 1.732 = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   Yes, parallel.
        *   So my vector derivation must be flawed.
        *   Where?
        *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{n} = 0$ condition for perpendicularity?
        *   No, $CD \perp$ plane means $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal.
        *   So we need $\vec{CD} \times \vec{n} = 0$ or $\vec{CD} = k \vec{n}$.
        *   Or $\vec{CD}$ is orthogonal to every vector in plane.
        *   Wait, if $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal, it is perpendicular to the plane.
        *   My derivation calculated $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{n}$?
        *   No, I calculated $D_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}) - C_{\perp} \cdot (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   This is dot product $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{n}$?
        *   $\vec{n} = \vec{u} \times D_{\perp}$.
        *   So yes, I calculated dot product.
        *   If dot product is non-zero, then $\vec{CD}$ is NOT perpendicular to normal?
        *   Wait.
        *   If vector $\vec{v}$ is parallel to normal $\vec{n}$, then $\vec{v} = k \vec{n}$.
        *   Then $\vec{v} \cdot \vec{n} = k |\vec{n}|^2 \ne 0$.
        *   So dot product being non-zero is consistent with being parallel.
        *   Dot product being zero would mean $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{n}$, i.e., $\vec{CD}$ lies in the plane.
        *   So my derivation showed dot product is non-zero, which supports $\vec{CD}$ being parallel to normal?
        *   No, dot product non-zero just means not perpendicular to normal. It could be at any angle.
        *   We need to check if $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to $\vec{n}$.
        *   Parallel means cross product is 0.
        *   Let's check cross product $\vec{CD} \times \vec{n}$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}$ (since parallel parts cancel).
        *   $\vec{n} = \vec{u} \times D_{\perp}$.
        *   Cross product: $(D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \times (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   Using vector triple product $(A \times B) \times C = (A \cdot C)B - (B \cdot C)A$.
        *   Wait, order. $(D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \times (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   Let $A = D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}$, $B = \vec{u}$, $C = D_{\perp}$? No.
        *   It is $X \times (Y \times Z) = (X \cdot Z)Y - (X \cdot Y)Z$.
        *   Here $X = D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}$, $Y = \vec{u}$, $Z = D_{\perp}$.
        *   Wait, $\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}$ is $Y \times Z$? No, $Y \times Z$ is $\vec{u} \times D_{\perp}$.
        *   Wait, standard identity: $A \times (B \times C) = (A \cdot C)B - (A \cdot B)C$.
        *   Here we have $X \times (\vec{u} \times D_{\perp})$.
        *   So $A=X, B=\vec{u}, C=D_{\perp}$.
        *   Result: $(X \cdot D_{\perp})\vec{u} - (X \cdot \vec{u})D_{\perp}$.
        *   $X \cdot D_{\perp} = (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \cdot D_{\perp} = |D_{\perp}|^2 - C_{\perp} \cdot D_{\perp} = 1 - 2 \cos \theta$.
        *   $X \cdot \vec{u} = (D_{\perp} - C_{\perp}) \cdot \vec{u} = 0$ (since $D_{\perp}, C_{\perp}$ perp to $\vec{u}$).
        *   So result is $(1 - 2 \cos \theta) \vec{u}$.
        *   For $\vec{CD}$ to be parallel to $\vec{n}$, the cross product must be 0.
        *   So $(1 - 2 \cos \theta) \vec{u} = 0$.
        *   Since $\vec{u} \ne 0$, we need $1 - 2 \cos \theta = 0$.
        *   $\cos \theta = 1/2$.
        *   $\theta = 60^\circ$.
        *   So, $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$ if and only if angle between projections is $60^\circ$.
        *   If angle is $120^\circ$, $\cos \theta = -1/2$, $1 - 2(-1/2) = 2 \ne 0$.
        *   So $CD$ is not parallel to normal.
        *   Wait, but earlier I checked with coordinates for angle 60 case, and it worked.

        *   $\cos \phi = \frac{\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2}{|\vec{n}_1| |\vec{n}_2|} = \frac{x_C x_D + y_C y_D}{\sqrt{x_C^2+y_C^2}\sqrt{x_D^2+y_D^2}}$.

        *   $|\vec{n}_2| = |D_{\perp}| = 1$.
        *   So $\cos \phi = \frac{C_{\perp} \cdot D_{\perp}}{2 \cdot 1} = \frac{2 \cdot 1 \cos \alpha}{2} = \cos \alpha$.
        *   So $\phi = \alpha$ (or $180-\alpha$).
